========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Doctor, I... I feel like I'm losing my mind a little bit, or perhaps my hearing is going, because I could have sworn you just asked me that! We really are just spinning our wheels today, aren't we? As I've been trying to say, I simply do not know. I haven't been a passenger since Arthur passed. 

Speaking of Arthur, though, did I ever tell you how we met? It was at a USO dance in 1955. He was in his Navy uniform, looking so handsome, and I was wearing this beautiful emerald green dress that my mother had made for me. She was a seamstress, you see, and she could copy any pattern she saw in a magazine. She made me this dress that looked just like one Grace Kelly wore. Anyway, they were playing Glenn Miller—"In the Mood," I think it was—and Arthur just marched right up to me and asked me to dance. He was a terrible dancer, bless his heart. He stepped on my toes at least three times, but he had this smile that just lit up the whole room. We went out for milkshakes afterward at this little diner downtown. It's not there anymore, of course. They tore it down in the nineties to build a parking garage. It's such a shame how they tear down all the beautiful old buildings these days. There used to be a magnificent movie theater on Main Street with a real Wurlitzer organ...

Oh, for heaven's sake. The USO dance and the movie theater. Doctor, please forgive me. I don't know why I can't seem to hold a thought in my head today. But truly, I just can't answer your question about being a passenger on a bridge, because I just haven't done it in years.

It's been so long since I haven't been the one behind the wheel, I couldn't rightly say how I'd feel.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience fear when you're a passenger in a car driving on a bridge?\n\nPatient: Well, doctor, like I was just saying... I tried everything to stop him—putting chicken wire down, sprinkling cayenne pepper—but nothing worked. He just loved the dirt. He used to come into the kitchen with mud all over his nose, wagging his tail so hard his whole body shook, and I couldn't even be mad at him...\n\nOh, dear me. Barnaby and the petunias. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Doctor, are you... are you quite alright? I'm starting to worry about you! I feel like I'm in one of those comedy routines on the television. I honestly don't know how else to say it—I haven't the faintest idea how I'd feel if someone else were driving, because I haven't let anyone drive me since Arthur. 

Though, you know, my niece Susan did offer to drive me to the grocery store last Thanksgiving. She has one of those new electric cars. Have you seen them? They don't make a sound! It's actually quite dangerous, if you ask me. I was walking across the parking lot at the pharmacy last week, and one of those silent little cars nearly backed right into me. The driver was looking at his telephone instead of the rearview mirror. People are always looking at their telephones these days. Back when I was a girl, we actually looked at each other when we walked down the street. We'd say 'good morning' and tip our hats—well, the men tipped their hats. My father had a beautiful felt fedora he wore every day to the bank. He was a teller there for forty years. Forty years! You just don't see that kind of loyalty to a company anymore. They gave him a gold watch when he retired, but it stopped working after about a month. We took it to the jeweler on 5th Avenue, Mr. Peterson, but he said the gears were completely...

Oh, dear. Goodness gracious me. Mr. Peterson and the gold watch. I am so sorry, doctor. I really am. I don't mean to be difficult, I just... I don't know the answer to your question. I just don't know.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, doctor, it's about my driving. What finally made me come in is... well, my daughter lives down in Miami now. She moved there back in, oh, it must have been 2015? She has this lovely little house with a mango tree in the backyard. The mangos are just enormous, you wouldn't believe it. I tried making a chutney out of them last summer, but I think I put too much ginger in it. My late husband always said I was heavy-handed with the ginger, bless him... \n\nBut anyway, what was I saying? Oh, right, the drive down there. The problem is that the moment my car reaches one of those long bridges toward Miami, my chest just tightens up completely. My hands clamp down on the steering wheel, and I get this sudden, absolute certainty that I have to get off the bridge right then and there, even though there’s nowhere to go. You're just stuck up there. And you know, the water under those bridges isn't even that pretty blue anymore, it's gotten so murky with all the construction and the boats... but yes, that feeling on the bridge is why I'm here.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience this kind of intense fear or anxiety in situations other than driving on bridges?\n\nPatient: No, no, it's really just those bridges. I mean, I'm perfectly fine doing my regular errands. I drive myself to the Publix every Tuesday morning without a second thought. Well, sometimes Wednesday if it's raining, my wipers are getting a bit old... but usually Tuesday. \n\nThey have the most wonderful bakery section there, you know. The young man behind the counter—Arthur, I think his name is, he wears those thick glasses—he always saves me a fresh loaf of the sourdough. It's getting so hard to find good sourdough down here. Most places just use a commercial mix, but you can really taste the difference when it's a proper starter. I actually tried making my own starter a few years ago when everyone on the television was doing it, but I must have left it out in the sun too long because it just smelled like old gym socks... \n\nBut anyway, I'm sorry, what were we talking about? Oh, right, being scared. No, it's just the bridges. Everywhere else I'm right as rain.\n\nDoctor: Is it the height of the bridges that scares you, or is it something else about them?\n\nPatient: Well, I don't think it's just how high up they are... it's more about all that open water underneath. \n\nYou know, Arthur—my late husband—he used to just love being out on the water. He bought this little Boston Whaler back in the eighties. Or was it the late seventies? No, it must have been the eighties because we had just put that awful orange shag carpet in the Florida room. I told him, Arthur, you cannot put orange carpet in a sunroom, the sun will just ruin it! And of course, it did. Within two years it faded to this dreadful peachy-yellow color that looked exactly like a bruised grapefruit. We ended up having to hire these two young men to rip it all up and put down tile. They tracked dust all over my good sofa... \n\nBut, oh dear, I'm rambling again, aren't I? What were you asking? Oh, right, the bridges. Yes, it's the water. I mean, I know perfectly well those bridges have been there for years and years, and plenty of people cross them every single day without a fuss. But when I think about driving over all that open water... the fear I get just feels so much bigger than the actual danger. It just doesn't make logical sense, but it just completely takes over.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear on the bridges related to worrying that something bad might happen, like the bridge collapsing or your car crashing into the water?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose that's part of it... I don't really know exactly what I'm afraid is going to happen. It's just this overwhelming feeling that something awful will. The thought of crashing into the water... well, I can't even swim, you see. \n\nI used to take swimming lessons when I was a little girl, back in Ohio. My mother insisted on it. She signed me up for these classes at the YMCA. The water was always freezing cold, and the instructor—Mr. Henderson, I think his name was—he had this very loud whistle that he would blow right next to my ear. It gave me terrible headaches. I told my mother, I said, 'Mama, I don't want to go back there, it smells like chlorine and wet feet.' But she made me go anyway. She always said a lady should know how to float, at the very least. I never did quite get the hang of floating, though. My legs always sank right to the bottom like two stones... \n\nOh, what was I saying? I'm sorry, I get so easily sidetracked these days. You were asking about what I'm afraid of on the bridge? To be honest, doctor, I don't even know if it's a specific thought like the bridge collapsing. It's just... I just don't know. The fear is just there, and it's so big I can't think straight.\n\nDoctor: Does this fear of driving on bridges happen every time you're on a bridge, or are there times when you can cross a bridge without feeling afraid?\n\nPatient: Every single time, yes. In fact, that's what finally made me come in to see you. Because the moment my car reaches one of those long bridges toward Miami... \n\nWell, my sister Beatrice lives down near Miami. Coral Gables, technically. She has this lovely little stucco house with a mango tree in the backyard. Oh, the mangoes she gets from that tree! They are just as sweet as candy. We used to make preserves out of them, boiling them down in a big copper pot with just a touch of ginger. You have to be so careful with ginger, though, it can just overpower everything if you aren't paying attention. I bought some ginger tea last week, thinking it might be nice before bed, but it was so terribly spicy I had to pour it straight down the sink. Such a waste of three dollars. They really don't make things the way they used to. \n\nBut, oh dear, I'm doing it again, aren't I? You asked if it happens every time. Yes, it does. Like I was saying, the second I'm on that bridge, my chest just tightens right up. My hands clamp onto the steering wheel so hard they ache, and I get this sudden certainty that I have to get off the bridge right that instant, even though there's nowhere to go but forward. It doesn't matter what day it is or how pleasant the drive was before that, it happens every single time.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually try to avoid driving on bridges because of this fear?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, I suppose I do try to avoid them now. It's gotten to the point where I've started turning down invitations to gallery openings, and even my birdwatching trips... \n\nOh, I used to love the birdwatching group. We'd go out early, early in the morning, when the dew was still on the grass. You have to wear the right shoes for that, though. I had this pair of sensible walking shoes, L.L. Bean, I think they were. Navy blue with a little white stripe. They were so comfortable, but then the sole just flapped right off one day while I was walking through the Publix parking lot! Can you imagine? I nearly tripped and broke my hip right there next to the shopping carts. A very nice young man who worked there helped me to my car. He had the most beautiful green eyes, just like my cousin Eleanor's. Eleanor married a dentist, you know. They moved to Chicago and she always complained about the wind...\n\nBut I'm sorry, I'm wandering off again, aren't I? You were asking about avoiding the bridges. Yes, I do. I even avoid visiting my own daughter sometimes, unless someone else can drive me over them. And it's just so embarrassing, doctor. I've always been the one who planned everything, the one who handled things myself. I have a college degree, for heaven's sake, I shouldn't be trapped on one side of the water just because I can't drive over a bridge! But I just can't bring myself to do it anymore.\n\nDoctor: Has your fear of driving on bridges affected your relationships with family or friends?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, I suppose it has. It makes me feel terribly disconnected. Just missing those senior-center exhibition meetings, for one. We were putting together a lovely watercolor exhibit there. I used to paint quite a bit of watercolor, you know. Mostly landscapes. I had this beautiful set of Windsor & Newton paints my late husband Arthur bought me for our thirtieth anniversary. \n\nWe went up to Cape Cod that year. The seafood there was just incredible, though I always found the New England clam chowder a bit too heavy on the cream. I much prefer a clear broth. Did you know some people put tomatoes in it and call it Manhattan style? That's just vegetable soup with clams in it, if you ask me. Arthur used to laugh so hard when I'd get worked up about the soup. He had such a wonderful, booming laugh... \n\nOh, dear me. I'm rambling about soup now, aren't I? I am so sorry, doctor. You asked about my relationships. Yes, well, between missing my meetings at the center and having to rely on others for my trips down to Miami, I just feel so much less independent. I feel entirely less connected to the activities and family responsibilities that used to structure my whole week. I'm just sitting at home now, waiting for someone else to offer me a ride, and I hate being a burden to them.\n\nDoctor: Do you worry that you might have a panic attack while driving on the bridge?\n\nPatient: Well, I... I don't know if I'd call it a panic attack, really. I just know that when I'm up there, suspended over all that water, I feel this terrible sense of losing control. I suppose I worry about what might happen if I just froze up right there in the middle lane. \n\nThe lanes on those new bridges are so narrow anyway, don't you think? They just squeeze you right in next to those enormous semi-trucks. I saw a documentary on public television about truck drivers once. It's a very lonely life, they said. Always eating at those rest stops. I stopped at a rest area on the turnpike a few years ago and tried to buy a simple egg salad sandwich from a vending machine. A vending machine! For egg salad! I wouldn't trust mayonnaise that's been sitting behind glass all day. My mother used to make her own mayonnaise from scratch. She'd stand at the counter with a whisk, just beating the oil in drop by drop until it was perfectly thick and glossy. You can't buy anything like that in a store today, it's all full of preservatives and chemicals that you can't even pronounce...\n\nOh, goodness, I've done it again. Mayonnaise. I am so sorry. You asked if I worry about having an attack. I just... I don't really know all the medical terms for it. I just know I'm terrified of getting stuck up there and not being able to breathe or steer my car properly.\n\nDoctor: Do you fear having symptoms like a racing heart or difficulty breathing when you're on a bridge?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, my heart does flutter quite a bit, like a little bird trapped in my chest. And it gets hard to catch my breath, almost like the air is too thin up there. It reminds me of when Arthur and I visited Denver. The Mile High City, they call it. \n\nWe took a train up through the Rockies, and the scenery was just spectacular. Snow on the peaks even in July! I bought a whole pound of the maple walnut. I've always loved maple flavoring. We used to tap our own trees when I was a little girl in Vermont. You have to boil down gallons and gallons of sap just to get a tiny little jar of syrup. My father would stay up half the night tending the boiler...\n\nOh, I'm doing it again, aren't I? The maple syrup. I'm sorry, doctor. You were asking about my heart and breathing on the bridge. Yes, they do act up terribly. My chest gets so tight, and my heart just races away. It's a very frightening feeling, not being able to just take a normal, deep breath.\n\nDoctor: Is it the sight of the bridge itself that triggers your fear, or is it more about the situation of being on the bridge?\n\nPatient: Well, it's really the moment I'm actually on it. What finally made me come in to see you is that the very second my car reaches one of those long bridges heading down toward Miami, my chest just tightens up entirely. My hands clamp down on the steering wheel like a vise, and I get this sudden, overwhelming certainty that I have to get off right then and there, even though there's absolutely nowhere to go but forward. \n\nMiami has changed so much, you know. I bought a whole pound of the maple walnut. I've always loved maple flavoring. We used to tap our own trees when I was a little girl in Vermont. You have to boil down gallons and gallons of sap just to get a tiny little jar of syrup. My father would stay up half the night tending the boiler...\n\nOh, I'm doing it again, aren't I? The maple syrup. I'm sorry, doctor. You were asking about my heart and breathing on the bridge. Yes, they do act up terribly. My chest gets so tight, and my heart just races away. It's a very frightening feeling, not being able to just take a normal, deep breath.\n\nDoctor: Is it the sight of the bridge itself that triggers your fear, or is it more about the situation of being on the bridge?\n\nPatient: Well, it's really the moment I'm actually on it. What finally made me come in to see you is that the very second my car reaches one of those long bridges heading down toward Miami, my chest just tightens up entirely. My hands clamp down on the steering wheel like a vise, and I get this sudden, overwhelming certainty that I have to get off right then and there, even though there's absolutely nowhere to go but forward. \n\nMiami has changed so much, you know. I remember when we first started going down there in the seventies. The Art Deco buildings were lovely, but some of them were looking a bit tired back then. Now they've painted them all those bright pastel colors—flamingo pinks and seafoam greens. I had a seafoam green dress once, for my niece Sarah's wedding. It was chiffon, with a lovely draped neckline. The tailor on 4th Street altered it for me. Mr. Rossi, his name was. He had this enormous gray cat that would just sleep on the bolts of fabric all day long. You'd think people would complain about the cat hair on their formal wear, but he was such a skilled tailor, nobody minded. He could take in a seam so perfectly you couldn't even tell the garment had been touched...\n\nOh, dear. Mr. Rossi's cat. I am so sorry, doctor. My mind just wanders off. You asked if it was the sight of the bridge or being on it. Yes, it's being on it. The moment the tires hit that span, that terrible panic sets in and I just feel trapped.\n\nDoctor: Is it the act of driving onto the bridge that scares you, or is it being in the middle of the bridge that's most frightening?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose it's both, really, but the middle is definitely the worst. Driving onto it is terrifying because you know what's coming, but once you're out there in the middle, suspended between the sky and the water... that's when I feel completely trapped. It's like being on an island with no boat. \n\nMy husband Arthur and I took a boat tour around the Thousand Islands once, up by the Canadian border. Have you ever been? It's just breathtaking. Some of the islands are so small they only have room for one tiny little house and a single tree. I always wondered how they got their groceries delivered. Do you think they have a special boat for the mailman? We had a mailman, Mr. Henderson, who delivered our mail for thirty years. He walked his route every single day, rain or shine, in those sturdy black oxfords. He always had a peppermint in his pocket for the neighborhood dogs. Our golden retriever, Buster, would sit by the front gate waiting for him every morning at ten o'clock sharp. Buster was such a good dog, although he did have a terrible habit of digging up my prize petunias...\n\nOh, my goodness. The petunias. I've done it again, haven't I? I am so sorry, doctor. You were asking about the middle of the bridge. Yes, being out in the middle is the absolute worst part. There's just no way off, and the panic is so strong I can barely grip the wheel.\n\nDoctor: Is it something about the way the bridge looks that scares you when you're driving onto it?\n\nPatient: Well, I'm not really sure it's the way it looks, exactly. I haven't really noticed anything specific about the design or the cables or anything like that. Actually, some bridges are quite beautiful to look at. Like the Golden Gate Bridge! Arthur and I went to San Francisco back in... oh, it must have been 1982. We rode one of those famous cable cars up the hills. The man driving it—the gripman, I think they call them—he was ringing the bell the whole way. Clang, clang, clang! \n\nAfterwards, we went down to Fisherman's Wharf and had clam chowder served right inside a hollowed-out loaf of sourdough bread. Have you ever tried baking sourdough, doctor? It is dreadfully complicated. I tried keeping a starter once, in a little glass mason jar on the kitchen counter. You have to feed it flour and water every single day, just like a demanding little pet. I even named mine 'Bubbles' because it got so frothy on top. But it smelled so sour, almost like old beer, and Arthur finally made me throw it out when it bubbled completely over the jar and made a terrible sticky mess all over my nice clean countertops. It took me an hour to scrub it off the tile grout...\n\nOh, dear me. Bubbles the sourdough starter. I am so sorry, doctor, my mind is just a sieve today. You were asking if the look of the bridge scares me. No, I don't think it's the appearance at all. I don't really know what it is, to be honest. It's just the knowing that I'm going up onto it.\n\nDoctor: Is it something about the bridge itself that triggers your fear, or is it the fact that you're driving on it?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose it's a bit of both, really. It's the bridge itself, being up so high with the water underneath, but also the fact that I'm the one driving. If I'm driving, I feel responsible, you know? Like I have to keep the car steady. \n\nMy father taught me to drive in an old Ford pickup truck out on the farm. It had a manual transmission, and the clutch was so stiff I had to use both feet to push it down. He would take me out to the back pasture where the cows were grazing. Those cows were so stubborn, they'd just stand right in the middle of the dirt path chewing their cud, completely ignoring us. We had one cow, a lovely black and white Holstein named Bessie, who gave the richest milk. My mother would use it to make homemade butter. She had this wooden butter churn, the kind with the plunger you have to pump up and down for what felt like hours. My arms would ache so badly by the time the cream finally separated. But oh, the taste of that fresh butter on a slice of warm, homemade bread... there was nothing quite like it. We'd have it with a little dollop of strawberry preserves that my grandmother canned every summer...\n\nOh, good heavens. Bessie the cow and the butter churn. I've done it again, haven't I? I am so, so sorry, doctor. You were asking if it's the bridge or the driving. I think... I think I'm just not entirely sure how to separate the two. It's the whole situation combined that just sets off that awful panic.\n\nDoctor: Is there something specific about the physical structure of the bridge that you see or experience when driving onto it that triggers your fear?\n\nPatient: Well, I don't believe it's any specific part of the structure, no. I've never really paid attention to the steel beams or the concrete or anything like that. \n\nAlthough, speaking of structures, my Arthur was quite the handyman when it came to building things. He built this magnificent cedar gazebo in our backyard back in... oh, it must have been the spring of '94. He drew up the blueprints all by himself at the dining room table. The smell of that fresh-cut cedar was just heavenly. We used to sit out there on warm summer evenings and drink sweet iced tea. I always put a sprig of fresh mint from my garden in the glasses. You have to be so careful with mint, though, doctor. It grows absolutely wild! I planted one tiny little pot of it, and within a year it had completely taken over my vegetable patch. It choked out my beautiful heirloom tomatoes. I was so heartbroken, because I had been planning to make my grandmother's famous marinara sauce. She brought the recipe all the way over from Naples, and it takes simmering on the stove for six solid hours to get the flavor just right, with lots of fresh garlic and olive oil...\n\nOh, for heaven's sake. The marinara sauce. I've done it again, haven't I? I am so terribly sorry, doctor, my mind just wanders off on its own. You were asking if I see a specific structure on the bridge that scares me. No, I really don't think so. I haven't noticed anything particular about how they're built. It's just the feeling of going onto them that does it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think it's the way the bridge looks that scares you when you're driving onto it?\n\nPatient: Well, doctor, I feel like we might be going in circles a little bit today! As I was saying, no, I really don't think it's the way it looks. I've never been one to pay much mind to the visual details of those things. \n\nMy sister Eleanor, on the other hand, she noticed everything. She was a watercolor painter, you see. Oh, she had such an eye for architecture and landscapes. Back in... oh, 1976, for the Bicentennial, we took a trip up the New England coast together. She wanted to paint the lighthouses. We stopped at this little antique shop in Maine because she was looking for a specific type of brush, and I ended up finding the most exquisite set of depression glass teacups. They were that lovely pale pink color—rosaline, I think the dealer called it. I brought them all the way home wrapped in my sweaters in my suitcase so they wouldn't chip. I used to host a ladies' card club every Tuesday afternoon, and I would serve Earl Grey tea in those pink cups. My friend Mildred always brought her famous lemon bars. They were perfectly tart, dusted with so much powdered sugar that if you laughed while taking a bite, you'd end up looking like a ghost! We laughed so hard one afternoon that Mildred spilled her tea right onto my good lace tablecloth...\n\nOh, bless my soul. Mildred and the lemon bars. I am so sorry, doctor, I'm doing it again. My mind just keeps taking the scenic route today. But truly, to answer your question, it isn't the sight or the look of the bridge that frightens me. I just don't know what else to tell you, it's just the whole ordeal of having to go over it.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of bridges something that you experience when you're a passenger in a car, or is it only when you're the one driving?\n\nPatient: Well, you know, it's funny you should ask that. I suppose I haven't really thought about it. I haven't been a passenger in a car since my Arthur passed away. He always drove us everywhere, you see. He had this magnificent old Cadillac, a '72 Coupe de Ville. It was the most beautiful shade of seafoam green. Oh, the seats were like sitting on a cloud! We used to take it down to Florida every winter to escape the cold. Arthur hated the snow. We'd pack up the trunk with all our things, and he'd always make sure to bring his golf clubs. He loved playing at this one course in Boca Raton, said the greens were the best he'd ever seen. I'd sit in the clubhouse and read my mystery novels while he played. I've always been partial to Agatha Christie. I think I've read *Murder on the Orient Express* at least half a dozen times. The way she weaves the clues together is just brilliant, don't you think? I always thought I would have made a rather good detective myself, what with my eye for detail. Why, just the other day I noticed Mrs. Higgins from down the street had a new mail carrier, and I said to myself...\n\nOh, good heavens. Agatha Christie and Mrs. Higgins' mail carrier. I am so terribly sorry, doctor. I've done it yet again, haven't I? You were asking if I get scared when I'm a passenger. I suppose the honest truth is, I just don't know anymore. It's been so long since I haven't been the one behind the wheel, I couldn't rightly say how I'd feel.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience fear when you're a passenger in a car driving on a bridge?\n\nPatient: Well, doctor, like I was just saying... I really couldn't tell you. It feels like we're just asking the same question over again, doesn't it? I haven't been in the passenger seat for years, not since my Arthur died. \n\nAlthough, you know, speaking of being a passenger, I remember when we took a train ride through the Rockies back in '82. Now that was something else entirely! We had a sleeper car, and the dining car had these heavy velvet curtains and real silver cutlery. I had the most wonderful prime rib for dinner. The waiter, a lovely young man named Thomas, told us he was working his way through college to become a veterinarian. He was so passionate about animals. I told him about my golden retriever, Barnaby. Oh, Barnaby was such a good boy, but he had a terrible habit of digging up my prize-winning petunias. I tried everything to stop him—putting chicken wire down, sprinkling cayenne pepper—but nothing worked. He just loved the dirt. He used to come into the kitchen with mud all over his nose, wagging his tail so hard his whole body shook, and I couldn't even be mad at him...\n\nOh, dear me. Barnaby and the petunias. I've gone and done it again, haven't I? I am so, so sorry, doctor. My brain is just a runaway train today! But yes, to answer your question, I just don't know if I'd be scared as a passenger on a bridge, because I simply haven't done it in such a long time.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if you've ever felt this fear when you're riding as a passenger on a bridge, or is it only when you're the one driving?\n\nPatient: Doctor, I... I feel like I'm losing my mind a little bit, or perhaps my hearing is going, because I could have sworn you just asked me that! We really are just spinning our wheels today, aren't we? As I've been trying to say, I simply do not know. I haven't been a passenger since Arthur passed. \n\nSpeaking of Arthur, though, did I ever tell you how we met? It was at a USO dance in 1955. He was in his Navy uniform, looking so handsome, and I was wearing this beautiful emerald green dress that my mother had made for me. She was a seamstress, you see, and she could copy any pattern she saw in a magazine. She made me this dress that looked just like one Grace Kelly wore. Anyway, they were playing Glenn Miller—\"In the Mood,\" I think it was—and Arthur just marched right up to me and asked me to dance. He was a terrible dancer, bless his heart. He stepped on my toes at least three times, but he had this smile that just lit up the whole room. We went out for milkshakes afterward at this little diner downtown. It's not there anymore, of course. They tore it down in the nineties to build a parking garage. It's such a shame how they tear down all the beautiful old buildings these days. There used to be a magnificent movie theater on Main Street with a real Wurlitzer organ...\n\nOh, for heaven's sake. The USO dance and the movie theater. Doctor, please forgive me. I don't know why I can't seem to hold a thought in my head today. But truly, I just can't answer your question about being a passenger on a bridge, because I just haven't done it in years.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would feel the same level of fear if you were riding as a passenger on a bridge, with someone else driving?\n\nPatient: Doctor, are you... are you quite alright? I'm starting to worry about you! I feel like I'm in one of those comedy routines on the television. I honestly don't know how else to say it—I haven't the faintest idea how I'd feel if someone else were driving, because I haven't let anyone drive me since Arthur. \n\nThough, you know, my niece Susan did offer to drive me to the grocery store last Thanksgiving. She has one of those new electric cars. Have you seen them? They don't make a sound! It's actually quite dangerous, if you ask me. I was walking across the parking lot at the pharmacy last week, and one of those silent little cars nearly backed right into me. The driver was looking at his telephone instead of the rearview mirror. People are always looking at their telephones these days. Back when I was a girl, we actually looked at each other when we walked down the street. We'd say 'good morning' and tip our hats—well, the men tipped their hats. My father had a beautiful felt fedora he wore every day to the bank. He was a teller there for forty years. Forty years! You just don't see that kind of loyalty to a company anymore. They gave him a gold watch when he retired, but it stopped working after about a month. We took it to the jeweler on 5th Avenue, Mr. Peterson, but he said the gears were completely...\n\nOh, dear. Goodness gracious me. Mr. Peterson and the gold watch. I am so sorry, doctor. I really am. I don't mean to be difficult, I just... I don't know the answer to your question. I just don't know."
